## rev0499 — priorities addendum for handoff-graph work
The broad ordering is unchanged.
What changed is that the repo can now say **what artifact edges should harden first between the top seams** instead of only saying which seams and shipsets are strongest.

Priority interpretation:
- **Build-State Evidence** still wins overall and now also anchors the first required receipt edge: Cargo raw facts → Build-State Pack.
- **Package Intake + Release Boundary Review** remains the sharpest urgent operator seam and now also anchors the strongest service-truth edge: crates.io/security truth → Intake Review Kit.
- **Compatibility Claims** stays behind the first build pair, but the handoff graph now makes its practical role clearer: structured API facts and witness programs flow into release-boundary review rather than living as a free-standing empire.
- **Feedback / Debug Acceptance Commons** remains a deepen lane and now has a cleaner upstream dependency: Build-State Pack → Debug Acceptance Matrix.
- **Safety-Critical + Institutional Readiness Commons** remains stewardship-heavy and now has a clearer bridge from local review receipts into readiness cards.
- **Adoption Navigation + Ecosystem Atlas** remains strategically important but downstream: it should consume reviewed receipts rather than invent defaults from atmosphere.

Refusal rule:
- do not answer “how do these fit together?” with a new platform shell when the archive can now name the producer, consumer, and receipt edge directly.

## rev0498 — priorities addendum for kernel-shipset work
The broad ordering is unchanged.
What changed is that the repo can now say **what exact first repo shape the top kernelized seams should take** instead of only saying which seams deserve energy.

Priority interpretation:
- **Build-State Evidence** still wins overall and now also has the clearest validated first shipset: a local-first capture/diff/doctor companion with explicit stable-only versus unstable-enhanced posture.
- **Package Intake + Release Boundary Review** remains the sharpest urgent operator seam and now also has the clearest first local review kit with route profiles, waivers, quarantines, and drills.
- **Feedback / Debug Acceptance Commons** remains a deepen lane and now has a cleaner shipset answer: fixture corpus + session packs + tuple cards rather than a debugger fork.
- **Safety-Critical + Institutional Readiness Commons** remains stewardship-heavy and now has a cleaner first shipset answer: validated readiness cards and playbooks rather than certification theater.
- **Adoption Navigation + Ecosystem Atlas** remains intentionally outside the shipset ledger for now because the blocker is renewal burden, not missing repo modules.

Refusal rule:
- do not answer “what should a small team build first?” with another platform program or portal dream when the archive already has a smaller kernel card.

## rev0497 — priorities addendum for decision-journey work
The broad ordering is unchanged.
What changed is that the repo can now say **which repeated decision moments the strongest seams should compose inside** instead of only which seams are individually strongest.

Priority interpretation:
- **Build-State Evidence** still wins overall and now also anchors the clearest first journey: build-regression triage over reviewable packs, diffs, and doctor posture.
- **Package Intake + Release Boundary Review** remains the sharpest urgent operator seam and now also anchors the clearest review journey: dependency admission and publish review with explicit route and waiver posture.
- **Feedback / Debug Acceptance Commons** remains a top deepen lane and now has a clearer cross-seam role as the entry to debug issue handoff rather than as a generalized debugger replacement.
- **Compatibility Claims** stays slightly behind the first build pair, but the release-boundary journey is becoming more concrete as official `cargo-semver-checks` work pulls closer to publish-time review.
- **Safety-Critical + Institutional Readiness Commons** remains the strongest stewardship-heavy journey, not because it is the easiest first build, but because the evaluation moment is real and repeated.
- **Adoption Navigation + Ecosystem Atlas** remains strategically important but should still stay narrow: its best current journey is conservative default selection with renewal receipts, not a giant recommendation portal.


## rev0496 — priority-routing addendum for launch wedges and first-proof paths
When the task is mainly **how a strong contribution should actually enter the world**, **what first user or maintainer it should help**, **what decision window should make the wedge real**, or **what proving ground should count as honest first proof**, route through the launch-wedge layer before you widen the seam, the product story, or the stewardship ask.

Immediate reading order:
- keep **Build-State Evidence** as the strongest single-project claim and now also as the clearest **first wedge**;
- keep **Package Intake + Release Boundary Review** as the clearest urgent operator-facing **publish/admission wedge**;
- keep **Feedback / Debug Acceptance Commons** and **Safety-Critical + Institutional Readiness Commons** as the strongest **deepen lanes**, now with clearer first-adopter proofs;
- keep **Compatibility Claims** as a second-wave release wedge that compounds over stronger evidence layers; and
- keep **Adoption Navigation + Ecosystem Atlas** strategically huge but wedge-constrained until renewal burden is solved more honestly.

Refusal rule:
- do not let a promising broader product story outrun the first believable wedge that would actually earn it.

## rev0495 — priority-routing addendum for hypothesis-ledger / falsifier-gate work
When the task is mainly **which broad present-tense claims still deserve confidence**, **what would narrow them**, or **what should stop being repeated**, route through the hypothesis ledger before you rewrite the ladder, the packets, or the frontier story.

Immediate reading order:
- keep **Build-State Evidence** as the strongest single-project claim unless its ledger card narrows;
- keep the strongest coordinated answer as a **thin evidence/review/readiness family**, not a platform empire;
- keep **Package Intake + Release Boundary Review** as the clearest urgent operator-facing pair;
- keep **Feedback / Debug Acceptance** and **Safety-Critical + Institutional Readiness** as deepen lanes unless their claim cards materially change; and
- keep fast substrate movement as a **watchcard-first** event unless multiple independent signals actually break the canon claim.

Refusal rule:
- do not let a fresh source or a smooth summary silently change a strong claim if the corresponding hypothesis card still says otherwise.

## rev0494 — priority-routing addendum for hot substrate watchcards
When the task is mainly **what changed in fast-moving upstream/service/machine-readable truth and how should that affect the repo without a rerank**, route through the new watchcard layer before you touch packets or canon.

- keep **Package Intake + Release Boundary Review** second overall, and treat it as the clearest **incident/operator hot loop + packet** seam;
- keep **Feedback / Debug Acceptance Commons** as the clearest **monthly packet + quarterly widening** seam;
- keep **Safety-Critical + Institutional Readiness Commons** as the clearest **quarterly stewardship** seam;
- keep **Compatibility Claims** as a **release- and drift-driven packet** seam; and
- keep **Adoption Navigation + Ecosystem Atlas** on the slowest **renewal and canon** loop because renewal burden remains the main blocker.

Refusal rule:
- do not let a hot source change rewrite the broad ladder when a packet refresh, charter refresh, or hygiene refresh is the honest smaller move.

## rev0491 priority addendum — shared operating surface is the next missing family layer
The broad priority order is unchanged.

Immediate emphasis:
- keep **Build-State Evidence** first, and use it as the clearest proving ground for `report`, `diff`, `explain`, `doctor`, and `export` discipline;
- keep **Package Intake + Release Boundary Review** second, and sharpen it around `review`, `admit`, `quarantine`, `waive`, and `recheck` verbs;

- keep **Compatibility Claims** as the clearest renewable-claim lane; and
- treat **Tooling Contract / Semantic Context** as an enabling import/export substrate for the family rather than a standalone framework empire.

Refusal rule:

## Current frontier note (rev0412)
### Promote the toolchain productization contract as the next toolchain-variant / sysroot / runtime-analysis-shaping move
The broad ladder is unchanged, and the archive did **not** need another top-band reorder.
What it needed was a sharper statement that **provisioned toolchain identity, stdlib/sysroot profile identity, activation/reuse truth, instrumented-runtime truth, and later release/safety/support claims are not the same seam**.

The clearest next move is now:
- keep **Workspace Environment Contract** as the explicit **setup / realization / observation / handoff** seam;
- keep **Cargo build interop contract** as the explicit **workspace-graph / plan / event / adapter** seam;
- keep **Compatibility Claims** and **Safety-Critical Assurance** as downstream claim/evidence consumers;
- keep **Cross Toolchain Kit + Sysroot Pack Kit + Sanitizer Battery Kit** as the lower-layer substrate;
- but promote **Toolchain Productization Stack**, read now as a **Toolchain Productization Contract**, as the clearest next **toolchain-variant / sysroot / runtime-analysis-shaping** move for provisioning facts, stdlib/sysroot profile families, activation/reuse posture, runtime-analysis lanes, and consumer handoffs.

Why this is the right move now:
- the 2026 flagships keep **Building blocks** centered on rebuild-std work and larger-build-system integration;
- the accepted `build-std` goal is about a stabilizable MVP rather than indefinite experimentation;
- Cargo's current unstable `-Z build-std` flow still requires `rust-src`, nightly Cargo, nightly rustc, and passing the flag to all invocations;
- rustup still splits toolchains, targets, profiles, components, and directory-sensitive overrides into separate surfaces;
- rustup cross-compilation still says target stdlib install is not enough because linkers and SDKs are often required;
- custom-target docs still require compiler pinning because target JSON remains unstable;
- sanitizer docs and sanitizer-stabilization work both reinforce the need for instrumented standard-library families; and
- Rust for Linux plus the CPython conversations prove the pressure is no longer niche.

Immediate priority order:
1. keep **Build-State Evidence** as the strongest broad/buildable epic contribution overall;
2. keep **Adoption Navigation Contract** as the strongest explicit **anti-tacit-knowledge / project-scoped recommendation** frontier overall;
3. keep **Reviewable Lane Defaults + renewal receipts** as the immediate execution seam beneath that frontier;
4. keep **Debuggability** high as the rising cross-cutting frontier;
5. keep **Rust inner-loop contract / Feedback Loop Stack** as the clearest next **bundle-shaping** move beneath the build/debug band;
6. keep **Workspace Environment Stack / Workspace Environment Contract** as the clearest next **environment / handoff-shaping** move;

9. keep **Publisher & Source Identity Contract** as the clearest next **publication / authority / route-shaping** move;
10. keep **Distribution Contract** as the clearest next **delivery / acquisition / ownership-shaping** move;
11. keep **Maintenance Reality Contract** as the clearest next **stewardship / continuity / support-routing** move;
12. keep **Defect Escalation Stack / Defect Escalation Contract** as the clearest next **upstream-routing / repro-to-regression-handoff** move;
13. keep **Benchmark Evidence Kit / Benchmark Evidence Contract** as the clearest next **performance-claim / compare-shaping** move;
14. keep **Compatibility Claims** as the clearest next **support/claim-shaping** move;
15. keep **Proc-Macro Exit Stack** as the clearest next **compile-time/transition-shaping** move;
16. keep **Cargo artifact contract** as the clearest next **final-output / handoff-shaping** move;

- keep it explicitly adjacent to, but distinct from, **Workspace Environment**, **Cargo build interop**, **Compatibility Claims**, **Safety-Critical Assurance**, and **Firmware / Release** imports;
- do not flatten one rustup override, one `build-std` invocation, one custom target JSON, one sysroot cache, or one sanitizer CI lane into the whole toolchain story; and

- the rustc-dev-guide says bug fixes should come with succinct regression tests, which means escalation should anticipate testability rather than stopping at prose;
- the libtest-JSON goal is explicitly about moving reporting responsibility upward toward Cargo/runners, which supports richer escalation imports;
- Cargo 1.94 keeps making local build evidence more machine-usable through `cargo report rebuild` and `cargo report sessions`; and

## Current frontier note (rev0406)

Why this is the right move now:
- crates.io now supports Trusted Publishing across multiple CI providers, allows Trusted-Publishing-only mode, and blocks risky GitHub Actions triggers;

- Cargo’s registry-authentication docs make credential-provider posture part of authenticated alternate-registry behavior;
- Cargo’s source-replacement docs explicitly define replacement in terms of exact-copy equivalence rather than “another registry”; 
- RFC 3243 plus the active open-namespaces goal keep namespace control a live implementation seam;
- RFC 3052 makes it impossible to keep treating manifest authorship, current ownership, and user-facing identity as the same plane; and
- `cargo package` explicitly says packaging-time VCS hints are not verified provenance.

## Current frontier note (rev0401)
### Promote the reviewable edit contract as the next mutation / review / handoff-shaping move
The broad ladder is unchanged, and the archive did **not** need another top-band reorder.
What it needed was a sharper statement that **code-mutation truth is not the same seam as compiler facts, semantic context, editor sessions, or assistant proposals**.

The clearest next move is now:

Why this is the right move now:
- the Cargo Book says `cargo fix` automatically applies `rustc` suggestions, but only over the configuration actually checked, which makes subject/config identity part of the edit story;
- the Edition Guide says migration can require repeated passes, `--broken-code`, and manual handling for macros, generated code, or doctests, which proves candidate discovery, selection, application, and verification are not one step;
- Cargo 1.90 explicitly says the current `cargo fix` architecture is slow, applies only a subset of lints, and is hard to make selective or interactive because of its `rustc`-proxy design and locking;
- the GSoC 2025 `cargo-fixit` prototype shows a top-level controlled and more interactive design is feasible;
- Cargo 1.93 says schema work could unblock a faster, more flexible fix architecture;
- the StableMIR publication goal shows Rust wants semver-governed public compiler-facing crates for analyzers, linters, and development environments;
- and the 2025 State of Rust survey says online docs remain canonical while editors with agentic support are rising, which strengthens the case for a governed edit boundary instead of direct assistant mutation.

Immediate priority order:

2. keep **Adoption Navigation + reviewable defaults + receipts** as the strongest anti-tacit-knowledge answer overall;
3. keep **Debuggability** high as the rising cross-cutting frontier;

5. keep **Workspace Environment Stack / Workspace Environment Contract** as the clearest next **environment / handoff-shaping** move;
6. keep **Package Intake Gateway** as the clearest newly sharpened **supply-chain/intake-shaping** seam;

## Current frontier note (rev0400)
### Promote the workspace environment contract as the next environment / handoff-shaping move
The broad ladder is unchanged, and the archive did **not** need another top-band reorder.

- Cargo workspace discovery also walks parent directories and changes package-selection defaults based on current working directory and workspace-root posture;
- rustup override selection also walks the directory tree and `rust-toolchain.toml` can declare channel/components/targets/profile, which proves toolchain pinning is real but incomplete environment truth;
- Cargo 1.94 explicitly says broken parent manifests or `.cargo/config.toml` files can fail unrelated builds and says broader workspace/config discovery behavior still needs improvement;
- rust-analyzer already exposes override commands, invocation strategies, extra args/env, linked-workspace behavior, and dedicated target-dir tradeoffs, which proves editor reality and Cargo reality still need an honest shared handoff;

- the Rust Foundation’s 2026–2028 strategy explicitly couples stable infrastructure, sustainable maintenance, and adoption growth, which is exactly where environment handoff truth lives.

10. keep **Cargo build interop contract** as the clearest next **workspace-graph / plan / event / adapter-shaping** move beneath Cargo plumbing, rust-analyzer discovery, and larger-build-system integration pressure;

## Current frontier note (rev0399)

## Current frontier note (rev0395)

Why this is the right move now:
- Cargo’s March 21, 2026 security advisory says a vulnerability in the `tar` crate used by Cargo to extract packages during a build can let a malicious crate change permissions on arbitrary directories, and it explicitly calls out alternate-registry users to verify impact with their vendor;
- Cargo source replacement and alternate-registry docs already make route semantics explicit, but the ecosystem still lacks one portable artifact boundary for route + payload + local staging truth;
- `cargo package` already models normalized manifests, default lockfile inclusion, flattened symlinks, pristine extraction/build verification, and best-effort-only VCS metadata, which means package ingress is already a real contract surface rather than an implementation footnote;
- `cargo install` still has materially different lockfile, target-dir, and config-discovery behavior, proving that “package intake” and “consumer install” are adjacent but not identical layers;
- crates.io’s January 2026 update added Trusted-Publishing-only mode and `pubtime`, while the February 2026 malicious-crate policy update pushes routine malware communication toward RustSec advisories, increasing the value of durable local intake artifacts rather than ambient announcement watching;
- and sandboxed build scripts matter, but they govern compile-time execution **after intake**, not the route/extraction/staging boundary itself.

Immediate priority order:
1. keep **Build-State Evidence** as the strongest broad/buildable epic contribution overall;
2. keep **Adoption Navigation + reviewable defaults + receipts** as the strongest anti-tacit-knowledge answer overall;

The archive already had a strong abstract **SDK Productization Stack**, a **publishable library** card, and a **polyglot component** card, but it still lacked a maintained answer to a central practical question: what should a serious public Rust SDK family normalize around right now when the real source of truth is an OpenAPI-described API and the release/support story has to survive regeneration?

Why this is the right correction now:
- Rust’s latest challenges framing still says ecosystem navigation is burdened by **choice paralysis** and **tacit knowledge**;
- the 2025 State of Rust survey still says docs are canonical while editor/LLM mediation rises;
- `utoipa` and `progenitor` now make the Rust-native OpenAPI producer/consumer lane concrete enough to support a bounded default;
- OpenAPI Generator’s Rust lane remains a serious alternative, which makes a narrower Rust-native boring default more valuable than another generic comparison;
- `smithy-rs` and the AWS SDK for Rust keep proving that generated SDK families are real products with configuration, examples, and runtime layering rather than mere stubs;
- Oxide’s SDK/CLI repo remains the clearest practical Rust-native blueprint: one checked-in OpenAPI source, checked-in generated code, CI synchronization, and explicit versioning;
- Cargo/docs.rs/crates.io now expose enough semver/docs/publication/review surface to make public SDK families inspectable products.

Immediate priority order:
1. keep the new **public SDK family** card and receipt current;
2. keep **publishable library**, **public SDK family**, and **polyglot workspace component** distinct instead of drifting back into one generic “Rust crate/library/client” bucket;
3. return to renewal-first discipline for the oldest central card whose lane-level answer materially changed;
4. only then consider another new public card such as **portable self-hosted Wasm edge host**, **native-shell mobile + UniFFI**, or **browser + Node dual-target Wasm package overlay**.

Preferred shape:
- public SDK family lane = **source-contract truth + generated-code truth + hand-written overlay truth + runtime/auth/config truth + docs/example/mock/CLI family truth + release/versioning/package truth + support/docs truth**;
- do not flatten this lane into generic publishable-library advice;
- do not silently widen it into Smithy-first, protobuf-first, or internal-only generated-client claims.

The strongest next correction after the three-way public-web split was no longer another web note. It was the missing **mobile-first app product** card. - Cargo install already defines install-root precedence, source classes, reinstall triggers, packaged-lock behavior, and a special config-discovery boundary for non-`--path` installs;
- Cargo uninstall uses the same root-precedence model but only removes packages installed with `cargo install`, which makes ownership scope central;
- Cargo package says `--exclude-lockfile` is not for general use because some consumers expect a lockfile, including `cargo install --locked`;
- Cargo’s source-replacement docs and cargo vendor docs keep exact-copy mirrors, vendored read-only trees, and divergent local carries intentionally distinct;
- Cargo’s 1.86 development update explicitly says installed-binary update support is still outside Cargo proper, which means a portable continuity receipt chain is still missing; and
- the archive already has the leaf notes, so the stronger move is now **bundle shaping** rather than another adjacent seam.

Preferred design shape:
- managed subject → current managed state → typed lifecycle events (`INSTALL`, `UPDATE`, `ROLLBACK`, `UNINSTALL`, `ROUTE_CHANGE`) → explicit ownership scope → bounded downstream handoffs.
- Prefer a thin `cargo lifecycle` / `consumer-lifecycle-pack/v0` layer over a universal updater, package-manager abstraction layer, or app-store wrapper.

## Current frontier note (rev0373)
Treat **Epic Contribution Ladder 2026** (`design/epic-contribution-ladder-2026.md`) as the immediate anti-sprawl frontier above many adjacent seam notes. The archive should now distinguish **research priority** from **build priority** and default to sharpening the most buildable near-term companion-tool band: **Build-State Evidence**, **Workspace Environment**, **Package Admission**, **Consumer Lifecycle Continuity** (Consumer Install + Update Continuity + Distribution Contract + Distribution-Route Mobility), and **Toolchain Productization**.

Why this now matters:
- Rust’s March 20, 2026 challenges post says ecosystem navigation still depends too much on tacit knowledge and choice paralysis;
- the 2025 State of Rust survey says docs remain canonical while editor/LLM-mediated workflows are rising;
- Rust’s December 2025 vision work argues for better crate-level supportive interfaces and broader build-workflow extensibility;
- the 2026 flagship goals keep cargo-script, public/private dependencies, SBOM support, and build-std / larger-build-system integration in the active top band;
- Cargo 1.94 says workspace/config discovery remains brittle, the March 2026 build-dir-layout testing call shows tools still depend on internals, and Cargo 1.86 explicitly says installed-binary update support is still outside Cargo proper;
- the Rust Foundation’s 2026–2028 strategy couples stable infrastructure, sustainable maintenance, and adoption.

Preferred design shape:
- one ranked ladder for **what is most worth building now**;
- explicit MVP shape, artifact boundary, and non-goals for each top candidate;
- a bias toward improving the buildability of Tier A candidates before minting another top-band seam note.
- Prefer a thin **epic-candidate ladder** over another isolated frontier note when the archive already understands the seam but still has not chosen the buildable implementation path.

## Current frontier note (rev0372)
Treat **Distribution-Route Mobility Stack** (`design/distribution-route-mobility-stack.md`) as the next cross-cutting source/distribution seam worth sharpening beside **Publisher & Source Identity**, **Distribution Contract**, **Airgap**, **Fork Continuity**, and **Sunset Transition**, above **registry-switch / mirror-config / vendored-copy / local-registry / `[patch]` carry / multiple-location bridge** routes.

Why this now matters:
- Cargo’s registries docs make alternate registries a first-class route, let `package.publish` restrict allowed publish homes, and recent release notes say `cargo publish` will use an alternate registry by default when it is the only allowed target;
- Cargo’s source-replacement docs say replacement sources must be **exact copies**, may not add new crates, and are not the same thing as patching or a private registry;
- Cargo’s dependency docs say crates.io packages cannot depend on code published outside crates.io, while **multiple locations** let local `git`/`path` work bridge to a registry version later;
- `cargo vendor` says vendored sources are read-only and directs actual modifications toward `[patch]` or local `path`, so local copy and divergent carry already have different official semantics;
- crates.io’s January 2026 update expanded Trusted Publishing and Trusted-Publishing-only mode, making publish-home and publish-authority shifts more explicit;
- the Rust Foundation’s 2026–2028 strategy explicitly names **signed crates** and **official mirrors**, making route plurality part of Rust’s infrastructure strategy; and
- Rust’s March 2026 challenges post says ecosystem navigation still depends too much on tacit knowledge and choice paralysis, which gets worse when route changes hide in config, CI, or release prose.

Preferred design shape:
- route subject → current route class → exact-copy-or-divergent posture → publish/consumer constraints → route-change intent → bounded install/continuity/incident/sunset handoff.
- Prefer a thin `cargo route` / `route-pack/v0` layer over another registry implementation, mirror manager, vendoring helper, or migration dashboard.

## Current frontier note (rev0371)
Treat **Sunset Transition Stack** (`design/sunset-transition-stack.md`) as the next cross-cutting lifecycle/governance seam worth sharpening beside **Lifecycle Ledger**, **Succession Continuity**, **Fork Continuity**, **Migration Kit**, and **Trust Decision**, above **deprecated-badge / selective-yank / repo-archived / last-good-line / successor-README / unmaintained-advisory** routes.

Why this now matters:
- Cargo’s manifest docs still define maintenance states like `passively-maintained`, `as-is`, `looking-for-maintainer`, and `deprecated`, but they also say crates.io no longer displays badges directly, so Rust has lifecycle vocabulary without a strong shared review surface on the main registry;
- Cargo’s publishing docs say yanks do **not** delete code and existing lockfiles continue to work, so “we do not support this anymore” is different from “this disappeared”;
- RFC 3660 keeps deletions tightly constrained, which means many real Rust artifacts will remain in the registry archive long after maintainers want users to stop selecting them by default;
- RFC 3646 says unreachable-owner transfer mediation should stop and many continuity cases must pick a different name, so sunset often borders but should not be confused with succession or fork-continuation routes;
- the Rust Foundation’s 2026–2028 strategy plus the maintenance writeup say maintenance is ongoing, invisible, and burnout-sensitive, which makes support narrowing or retirement a legitimate stewardship act rather than automatic neglect; and
- Rust’s March 2026 challenges post says ecosystem navigation still depends too much on tacit knowledge and choice paralysis, which gets worse when sunset state lives only in READMEs, yanks, or rumor.

Preferred design shape:
- sunset subject / narrowing-or-retirement intent → last-supported-line and support-window truth → replacement-or-no-successor posture → registry/archive action posture → consumer off-ramp → closure witness / re-entry-or-terminal-state.
- Prefer a thin `cargo sunset` / `sunset-pack/v0` layer over another badge scheme, graveyard site, or stealth deprecation score.

## Current frontier note (rev0370)
Treat **Fork Continuity Stack** (`design/fork-continuity-stack.md`) as the next cross-cutting governance/distribution seam worth sharpening beside **Lifecycle Ledger**, **Succession Continuity**, **Publisher & Source Identity**, **Migration Kit**, and **Trust Decision**, above **successor-map / org-fork / temporary-fork / renamed-crate / `[patch]` carry / alternate-registry** routes.

Why this now matters:
- RFC 3646 says the crates.io team wants to stop mediating crate transfers, and unreachable-owner cases should expect to pick a different name instead of relying on registry arbitration;
- crates.io policy still requires explicit current-owner approval for ownership transfer, so “continuity” and “transfer” are not the same thing;
- Cargo’s overriding-dependencies docs make `[patch]` a first-class local carry lane, while source replacement explicitly says exact-copy mirrors are **not** patching or private-registry divergence;
- Cargo’s registries and dependency docs make alternate registries and multiple-location bridging real routes, but crates.io packages still cannot depend on code from outside crates.io;
- the Rust Project’s crate-ownership policy explicitly names an **Expatriated** category, proving continuation outside the original official owner is already a real concept; and
- the Rust Innovation Lab plus Rust’s 2026 challenges post together say that neutral-home relocations and tacit-knowledge-heavy successor choices are now ecosystem-scale problems rather than edge cases.

Preferred design shape:
- origin subject / continuation intent → authority-and-identity relationship → distribution route → compatibility-and-migration posture → continuity witness / reconvergence-or-exit.
- Prefer a thin `cargo continuation` / `continuation-pack/v0` layer over a forced-transfer service, fork marketplace, or universal successor badge.

Why this now matters:

- the Maintainers Fund announcement says support should be reliable, accountable, and aligned with Rust Project priorities rather than improvised;
- the maintenance writeup says maintenance work is broad and ongoing, so invisible release/ops knowledge is exactly what continuity transitions tend to lose first;
- the October 2025 program-management update says some Rust teams rely on one or two people simply to stay afloat;
- crates.io policy requires explicit current-owner approval for ownership transfer, which means succession and authority change cannot be guessed from inactivity;
- crates.io’s Trusted Publishing enhancements mean publish authority now often lives in CI/OIDC posture as much as in human intention; and
- the Rust Innovation Lab proves there is a real continuity lane above simple co-maintainership: a stable neutral home, governance support, legal/admin backing, and fiscal sponsorship for keystone infrastructure.

Preferred design shape:
- continuity subject / transition intent → authority surfaces → overlap/transfer plan → institutional anchor → continuity witness / escalation handoff.

## Current frontier note (rev0368)

Why this now matters:
- the Rust Foundation’s 2026–2028 strategy explicitly frames stable infrastructure, sustainable maintenance, adoption, and community support as one connected three-year program rather than separate nice-to-haves;
- the Maintainers Fund announcement says support should be transparent, accountable, aligned with Rust Project priorities, and aimed at long-term maintainer roles and continuity;

## Current frontier note (rev0367)
Treat **Deviation Governance Stack** (`design/deviation-governance-stack.md`) as the next cross-cutting control-plane seam worth sharpening beside **Institutional Overlay**, **Preview Adoption**, **Canary Validation**, **Baseline Ratchet**, and **Ecosystem Incident Response**, above **Release Truth / Support Envelope / Trust Decision / local policy consumer** routes.

Why this now matters:
- the rustc lint docs already distinguish temporary/self-checking suppressions (`expect`) from other lint posture, which proves some exceptions are meant to clear rather than linger silently;
- the Edition Guide’s advanced migrations docs treat partial carries, repeated runs, and configuration-specific migration passes as ordinary Rust migration reality rather than an embarrassing edge case;
- nightly/unstable docs make preview activation explicit and per-project, which means preview use is already a governed deviation path rather than a hidden implementation detail;
- Cargo’s override docs make `[patch]` a first-class way to carry local fixes or unpublished upstream work, while Cargo config docs warn that config-local patches are easy to hide because they usually are not checked into source control and inherit through parent directories;
- source replacement and patching carry different semantic promises, so not every “override” means the same thing;
- Cargo’s publishing docs say yanks do not fix existing lockfiles, which means exceptional carries can outlive upstream corrective action;
- Cargo’s CI guide shows that allow-fail or scheduled watch lanes are already real design choices with notification/ownership consequences; and
- Cargo’s `rust-version` docs say policy drift causes users to infer promises you did not intend, which makes explicit deviation records more important.

Preferred design shape:
- normative basis → deviation subject → mechanism → scope → owner/justification → expiry/watch → exit handoff.
- Prefer a thin `cargo deviate` / `deviation-pack/v0` layer over another policy wiki, giant waiver spreadsheet, or hidden compliance engine.

## Current frontier note (rev0366)
Treat **Ecosystem Incident Response Stack** (`design/ecosystem-incident-response-stack.md`) as the next security/change-management seam worth sharpening beside **Trust Decision**, **Package Admission**, **Canary Validation**, **Release Truth**, and **Distribution Contract**, above **Support Envelope / Update Continuity / local platform overlay** consumer routes.

Why this now matters:
- the January 2026 crates.io update makes incident-relevant registry signals and controls more first-class by adding a Security tab, expanding Trusted Publishing, enabling Trusted-Publishing-only mode, and blocking risky GitHub triggers;
- the February 2026 malicious-crate notification-policy update says routine malware removals will always flow through RustSec advisories, while broad public blog posts become the exception rather than the default;
- the Rust Foundation’s 2026–2028 strategy centers secure infrastructure, signed crates, official mirrors, and sustainable crates.io/release operations;
- the Rust Foundation’s 2025 security update says provenance tracking is live, Typomania and Painter have matured, real-time scanning pilots are underway, a Rust-focused Capslock-style Cargo subcommand is being built, and TUF-based signed-metadata work is part of the roadmap;
- Rust’s 2026 flagships keep secure-supply-chain work active through public/private dependency control and SBOM support; and
- Cargo’s publishing and override docs make clear that registry action alone is not containment: yanks do not fix existing lockfiles, while `[patch]` is a real local remediation lane.

Preferred design shape:
- incident subject → exposure slice → containment actions → rebuild/reissue truth → communication handoff → post-incident policy/drill import.
- Prefer a thin `cargo incident` / `incident-pack/v0` layer over another scanner wrapper, SOC dashboard, security score, or generic supply-chain manifesto.

Treat **Canary Validation Stack** (`design/canary-validation-stack.md`) as the next change-management seam worth sharpening beside **Preview Adoption**, **Baseline Ratchet**, **Migration Truth**, and **Cargo Report**, above **Compatibility Claims / Release Truth / Defect Escalation** consumer routes.

Why this now matters:
- The Rust book’s release-train appendix says Rust ships on a six-week train, with beta explicitly serving as the last station before stable and a place to catch regressions early.
- Cargo’s CI guide shows that watch lanes already have different semantics: nightly may be allow-fail, latest-deps verification is separate from lockfile determinism, and `rust-version` verification is a distinct job.
- Cargo’s `rust-version` docs say projects should choose and document a support policy and explicitly discuss dev-branch versus release-branch policy drift.
- Cargo’s resolver docs plus the CI guide’s latest-deps example show that “latest graph watch” and “declared floor watch” are different lanes with different dependency-selection semantics.
- Cargo’s future-incompat docs and `cargo report future-incompat` command make incoming hard-error warnings in dependencies replayable and machine-usable instead of transient console noise.
- Cargo config exposes `future-incompat-report.frequency`, proving that even notification behavior is already policy-shaped rather than an accident.
- The Cargo 1.94 cycle keeps turning report/session/rebuild data into more reusable evidence, making canary observations easier to attach to native artifacts.
- The 2025 State of Rust survey says most users stay on stable while nightly is mostly used out of necessity, which raises the value of seeing breakage early without laundering nightly into the whole project policy.

Preferred design shape:
- current baseline/support policy → selected canary lanes / cadence / coverage slices → gating and notification rules → observed canary outcomes → escalation to preview / ratchet / migration / dependency / defect lanes → bounded consumer handoff.
- Prefer a thin `cargo canary` / `canary-pack/v0` layer over another CI starter repo, dashboard, dependency bot, or one universal stable/beta/nightly policy.

## Current frontier note (rev0364)
Treat **Preview Adoption Stack** (`design/preview-adoption-stack.md`) as the next change-management seam worth sharpening beside **Baseline Ratchet**, **Migration Truth**, and **Workspace Environment**, above **Compatibility Claims / Support Envelope / Canonical Learning** consumer routes.

Why this now matters:
- The Rust book’s release-train appendix says unstable features land behind feature flags on nightly, stable/beta deliberately reject them, and beta is a real pre-stable testing lane.
- Cargo’s unstable-features docs say nightly-only features require explicit activation (`cargo-features`, `-Z unstable-options`, `-Z ...`) and should be followed through tracking issues.
- The Unstable Book says its docs are best-effort and may be out of date, which means projects need an explicit watch/landing posture rather than assuming unstable docs are the durable contract.
- The Edition Guide says unstable next-edition work can be tested on nightly with explicit `cargo-features`, may lack automatic migrations, and is not yet fully finished.
- The 2025 State of Rust survey says most people use stable while nightly is mostly used out of necessity; the 2024 survey says a substantial minority still use nightly for unstable features and beta is underused.
- Rust-for-Linux’s stable-support goals say unstable features have no reliability guarantee and can force pinned compiler versions, which makes preview usage a real support-policy seam.
- Active goals like scalable vectors and build-std are explicitly framed as nightly experiments or unstable MVPs intended to reach stabilization.

Preferred design shape:
- preview subject → activation/pin → adoption scope → guardrails / support boundary → watch/landing posture → stable-landing, quarantine-renewal, or removal handoff.
- Prefer a thin `cargo previewadopt` / `preview-pack/v0` layer over another feature-tracker portal, nightly distro, or generic “use stable unless you must” essay.

## Current frontier note (rev0363)
Treat **Baseline Ratchet Stack** (`design/baseline-ratchet-stack.md`) as the next change-management seam worth sharpening beside **Migration Truth**, **Compatibility Claims**, and **Lifecycle Ledger**, above **Release Truth / Update Continuity / support-policy** consumer routes.

Why this now matters:
- Rust 1.85 stabilized **Rust 2024**, and the Edition Guide still presents edition migration as a staged, configuration-sensitive workflow rather than one command.
- Cargo’s `rust-version` docs say changing `rust-version` is a minor incompatibility, recommend documenting a policy, warn against drift, and explicitly allow multiple policies in one workspace.
- Rust 1.84 stabilized MSRV-aware dependency selection, says resolver `3` can require a higher MSRV, and says the 2024 edition gets the new resolver behavior by default.
- The resolver docs say mixed-policy workspaces are only handled heuristically, so one member can force lower dependency versions for another or version unification can still force a higher shared version.
- Cargo’s CI guide now documents **verifying latest dependencies** and **verifying `rust-version`** as distinct checks.
- Cargo 1.94 says TOML 1.1 support can inadvertently raise the Cargo parser floor and explicitly points to verifying `rust-version` in CI.
- The 2025 State of Rust survey says developers mostly use stable and keep up with releases, which means baseline ratchets are part of ordinary Rust maintenance rather than an edge case.

Preferred design shape:
- current baseline vector → proposed ratchet delta → workspace/member variance → verification matrix → release-line policy → bounded migration/release/support handoff.
- Prefer a thin `cargo ratchet` / `baseline-pack/v0` layer over another upgrader, CI snippet, or “always latest Rust” policy page.

## Current frontier note (rev0362)
Treat **Iteration Profile Stack** (`design/iteration-profile-stack.md`) as the next local-execution seam worth sharpening beside **Feedback Loop** and **Workspace Environment**, above **Build Doctor / Debugger Experience** consumer routes, and in bounded contact with **Project Bootstrap / Async Transition / Tooling Contract**.

Why this now matters:
- Rust’s March 20, 2026 challenges post says compile times are a universal productivity tax and explicitly says that faster linking or hot reloading can have outsized impact on development velocity.
- The 2025 compiler-performance survey says the default `dev` profile’s full debuginfo slows compile/link loops, says many users have not tried easy performance knobs, and says Cargo is considering a lower-debuginfo default plus a built-in debugging profile.
- Cargo’s profile docs already expose real supported levers (`debug`, `split-debuginfo`, built-in and custom profiles, config overrides), so iteration tradeoffs are not just folklore.
- Cargo’s build-analysis work and `cargo report` direction make machine-usable evidence about rebuilds, flags, profiles, and sessions more available.
- The build-dir-layout work plus March 2026 testing call say editor/CLI coexistence and cache-layout assumptions are still live, tool-breaking seams.
- rust-analyzer now documents `cargo.targetDir` as an explicit lock-avoidance tradeoff that duplicates artifacts.
- The 2026 debugging survey says debugger quality still varies materially by debugger family / OS and that async debugging is not yet first-class.

Preferred design shape:
- workflow intent → evidence basis → chosen profile/config/editor realization → explicit debug/fidelity/concurrency tradeoffs → observed before/after consequences → bounded bootstrap/workenv/support import.
- Prefer a thin `cargo iterprofile` / `iter-profile-pack/v0` layer over another tips page, hidden editor preset, daemon, or one universal fast-dev profile.

Why this now matters:

Why this now matters:
- Rust’s March 20, 2026 challenges post says learning is background- and domain-dependent, recommends tailored learning paths, and says domain-specific materials help more than one generic tutorial.
- The 2025 State of Rust survey still says docs are canonical while editor/LLM-mediated learning is rising.
- Official vision work still says users need better ecosystem navigation, starter-set guidance, and better diagnostics/guidance from crates.
- `cargo script` keeps making tiny proofs, bug reports, and educational checkpoints cheaper to carry as first-route evidence.

Preferred design shape:
- background/domain profile → concept translation → canonical learning imports → lane choice → bootstrap/environment handoff → refreshable onramp pack.
- Prefer a thin `cargo onramp` / `onramp-pack/v0` layer over another tutorial site, fixed curriculum, or assistant-only guide.

### Sharpen Trait Surface with an explicit lane map instead of one fake “trait support” or “dyn support” verdict
Treat **Trait Surface Kit** as newly under-modeled in one specific way: the archive already had the right substrate for semantic obligations, dyn posture, return-shape truth, and migration metadata, but it still needed a clearer map of the **different trait lanes** Rust is actually using.

Why this now matters:
- Rust’s 2026 flagships explicitly keep **return type notation**, **async fn in dyn trait**, **next-solver stabilization**, and **evolvable trait hierarchies** on the active roadmap;

- the evolving-traits goal is directly about making `Receiver` a supertrait of `Deref` and splitting `tower::Service` into a non-`Sync` supertrait plus a `Sync` subtrait, which proves family evolution belongs in the review surface;
- RFC 3654 exists because trait methods returning opaques need explicit reviewable bounds like `Send`, while RFC 3185 explicitly left dyn safety for `async fn` traits to future work;
- docs.rs trait pages now visibly expose **Dyn Compatibility** on many traits, while `async-trait`, `dynosaur`, and `trait-variant` openly provide different present-tense adapter and split-family lanes rather than one converged answer;
- the 2025H2 in-place-initialization goal explicitly says dyn Trait for `async fn` and `-> impl Trait` relies on callee-initialized unsized returns, which ties trait-surface truth directly to initialization-surface truth; and
- the next-solver goal makes clear that some trait-family claims are still acceptance- and solver-sensitive rather than purely semantic.

### Sharpen Manifest Truth with an explicit lane map instead of one fake “the manifest” verdict
Treat **Manifest Truth Stack** as newly under-modeled in one specific way: the archive already had the right authored/published/consumer/discovery/script substrate, but it still needed a clearer map of the **different manifest lanes** Rust is actually using.

Why this now matters:
- Cargo 1.94 explicitly calls out parent-manifest and parent-config discovery hazards, which is direct evidence that authored file text and discovered interpretation are not the same lane;
- `cargo package` still rewrites and normalizes `Cargo.toml`, removes `[patch]`, `[replace]`, and `[workspace]`, always includes `Cargo.lock` unless excluded, and emits `.cargo_vcs_info.json`, which means packaged truth is already materially different from authored truth;
- `cargo metadata` remains the stable machine-readable consumer lane but still recommends pinning `--format-version`, while Cargo 1.94 changed `cargo info` to default to inspecting the local package when no registry is explicitly specified, which is direct evidence that machine and human consumer projections are distinct lanes;
- workspace inheritance now clearly spans `workspace.package`, `workspace.dependencies`, and `workspace.lints`, and parent-directory workspace discovery plus `package.workspace` override remain active semantics;
- cargo-script/frontmatter is now real nightly Cargo/rustc behavior rather than a hypothetical future, which means embedded manifest subjects must stop being treated as just funny package roots; and
- Rust 1.93 pulling out `cargo-util-schemas` as a crate is a useful reminder that schema extraction and final semantic truth are not the same thing.

Archive decision for this revision: add `design/manifest-surface-lane-map.md`; refresh `design/manifest-surface-kit.md`, `design/manifest-truth-stack.md`, `design/manifest-truth-pilot-program.md`, `gaps/manifest-truth-authored-published-and-consumed.md`, and `proposals/epic-manifest-truth-stack.md`; prepend a matching frontier note in `STRATEGIC_FRONTIER.md`; update `INDEX.md` and `RESEARCH_LOG.md`; and strengthen `AGENTS.md` plus `meta/AMNESIA_RESISTORS.md` so future manifest/repo-composition/script/publish revisions keep **authored package manifests, inherited/defaulted/discovered interpretation, packaged publish-normalized manifests, script/frontmatter subjects, machine-consumer imports, human projections, and evolving schema/watch lanes** distinct instead of narrating one fake manifest lane.


## Current frontier correction (rev0338)
### Sharpen Filesystem Surface with an explicit lane map and pilot program instead of one fake “filesystem support” verdict
Treat **Filesystem Surface Kit** as newly under-modeled in one specific way: the archive already had the right substrate for paths, traversal, staging, mutation, durability, and evidence, but it still needed a clearer map of the **different filesystem lanes** Rust is actually using.

Why this now matters:
- std `fs` now explicitly documents TOCTOU hazards, recommends atomic operations such as `File::create_new`, and says keeping files open for the duration of operations helps avoid some races;
- `canonicalize` still returns machine-correct canonical paths and on Windows still produces extended-length path syntax that may be awkward for command-line or human-facing use;
- nightly `std::fs::Dir` and `cap-std::fs::Dir` keep open-directory / rooted descendant operations distinct from ambient path lookup;
- `camino` keeps UTF-8 path posture explicit instead of burying it inside repeated `to_str().unwrap()` assumptions;
- `tempfile` and `atomic-write-file` prove that temp staging, replace posture, cleanup, and durability are already different lanes rather than one fake “atomic save” promise;
- `walkdir`, `ignore`, and `notify` keep traversal, ignore, deterministic-order, native-watch, and polling-watch behavior visibly plural; and
- `fs-mistrust`, `fs-err`, and `dunce` prove that trust/privacy checks, operation/path-rich diagnostics, and display-safe Windows path bridges are real filesystem-adjacent needs.

What to prefer next:
- keep **Filesystem Surface Kit** responsible for `fs-surface/v0`, `path-kind-profile/v0`, `resolution-traversal-profile/v0`, `mutation-durability-profile/v0`, `temp-staging-profile/v0`, `walk-watch-profile/v0`, `fs-adapter-profile/v0`, `fs-vector-set/v0`, `fs-check-report/v0`, `fs-pack/v0`, and the lane map in `design/filesystem-surface-lane-map.md`;
- keep **Runtime Capability Kit** responsible for what filesystem authority exists at all, rather than letting filesystem docs re-own ambient vs delegated authority from scratch;
- keep **Runtime Settings Kit** and **Command Surface Kit** responsible for config/source/CLI contracts above imported filesystem facts instead of letting path/save/watch docs quietly define the whole application surface;
- keep downstream security/product stacks importing bounded filesystem packs instead of each narrating their own hidden path/traversal/save semantics;
- prefer pilots that prove **save semantics first**, **path-kind/display bridges second**, **rooted/capability lanes third**, **traversal fourth**, **watch fifth**, and **trust/privacy lanes sixth**; and
- avoid another would-be universal path abstraction, watcher wrapper, or “safe fs” badge that hides path kind, rooted authority, traversal policy, watch backend, staging behavior, and durability meaning.

## Current frontier correction (rev0332)
### Sharpen Consumer Install with an explicit lane map and pilot program instead of one fake “install support” verdict
Treat **Consumer Install Kit** as newly under-modeled in one specific way: the archive already had the right leaf artifact family for subject/catalog/plan/receipt truth, but it still needed a clearer map of the **different install lanes** Rust is actually using.

Why this now matters:
- `cargo install` keeps the source-build lane sharp by ignoring packaged `Cargo.lock` unless `--locked` is passed and by tying install-root tracking plus concurrent-install protection to its metadata file;
- Cargo packaging docs now warn that `--exclude-lockfile` is not for general use because tools such as `cargo install --locked` may expect the packaged lockfile;
- `cargo-binstall` is explicit about its repository-release → quickinstall → alternate-target → `cargo install` fallback ladder and separately documents signature support;
- cargo-dist 0.31.0 makes host/mirror order part of installer behavior rather than hidden deployment trivia;
- `taiki-e/install-action` exposes manifest-managed installs, `cargo-binstall` fallback, `cargo install` fallback, checksum/signature posture, and even prerequisite package-manager setup, proving CI install is its own lane rather than a transparent wrapper;
- `axoupdater` shows receipt-driven downstream consumers are real by loading cargo-dist install receipts, but those receipts are still too tool-local to be the shared first-install contract;
- and crates.io’s Trusted Publishing / `pubtime` work sharpens producer truth without answering what any given user machine or runner actually installed.

Archive decision for this revision: add `design/consumer-install-lane-map.md` and `design/consumer-install-pilot-program.md`; refresh `design/consumer-install-kit.md`, `gaps/consumer-install-selection-channels-mirrors-and-receipts.md`, and `proposals/epic-consumer-install-kit.md`; prepend a matching frontier note in `STRATEGIC_FRONTIER.md`; update `INDEX.md` and `RESEARCH_LOG.md`; and strengthen `AGENTS.md` plus `meta/AMNESIA_RESISTORS.md` so future install/distribution/update/support revisions keep **source-build installs, prebuilt installs, mirror/host fallback, CI-wrapper installs, delegated/imported installs, and durable receipt/managed-content claims** distinct instead of narrating one fake universal install lane.

Treat **Update Continuity Kit** as newly under-modeled in one specific way: the archive already had the right substrate for install-state, candidate, plan/apply, and ownership artifacts, but it still needed a clearer map of the **different continuity lanes** Rust is actually using.

Why this now matters:

- the Cargo 1.86 development-cycle report still treats `cargo install-update` as a plugin rather than a built-in Cargo lane;

- `update-kit` plus `self-replace` keep detection/check/plan/apply and platform-sharp self-replace/uninstall semantics explicit;
- Cargo 1.90’s path-ownership work keeps uninstall scope and managed-content cleanup explicit instead of treating them as invisible postscript.

Archive decision for this revision: add `design/update-continuity-lane-map.md` and `design/update-continuity-pilot-program.md`; refresh `design/update-continuity-kit.md`, `gaps/update-continuity-detection-selection-apply-rollback-and-uninstall.md`, and `proposals/epic-update-continuity-kit.md`; and strengthen hygiene so future update/install/support revisions keep **source-installed tool updates, prebuilt reinstall flows, receipt-driven updater lanes, bundle/app updater lanes, delegated-manager posture, ownership/rollback/uninstall truth, and forensic/imported handoffs** distinct instead of narrating one fake universal update lane.

## Current frontier correction (rev0327)
### Sharpen Time Surface with an explicit lane map and pilot program instead of one fake “datetime support” verdict
Treat **Time Surface Kit** as newly under-modeled in one specific way: the archive already had the right substrate for clock/zone/calendar/storage/evidence artifacts, but it still needed a clearer map of the **different temporal lanes** Rust is actually using.

Why this now matters:
- current std docs still keep the foundational split sharp: `Instant` is a monotonically nondecreasing opaque clock for `Duration` math, while `SystemTime` is explicitly not monotonic and is for external entities like filesystems and other processes;
- the `time` crate still exposes a real civil/offset/wire lane through explicit formatting/parsing, `local-offset`, and `serde-human-readable` feature posture instead of pretending locale/zoned work is built in by default;
- Jiff’s current docs and comparison/platform notes keep the zoned lane unusually explicit through automatic TZDB integration, Windows TZDB embedding, and CLDR-backed Windows-to-IANA local-zone mapping;
- Chrono and Chrono-TZ keep proving that zoned time remains plural rather than settled: Chrono does not ship timezone data by default, while Chrono-TZ uses build-generated IANA zone implementations;
- ICU4X keeps localized date/time/zone rendering and field-set selection explicit rather than collapsing them into generic format strings;
- Tokio’s paused and advanced test time keep deterministic/fake-clock behavior explicit and runtime-scoped rather than making it look like ordinary wall or monotonic time.

Archive decision for this revision: add `design/time-surface-lane-map.md` and `design/time-surface-pilot-program.md`; refresh `design/time-surface-kit.md`, `gaps/time-surfaces-clocks-time-zones-and-calendrical-contracts.md`, and `proposals/epic-time-surface-kit.md`; and strengthen hygiene so future time/background-work/localization revisions keep **monotonic elapsed/timeout clocks, wall-clock/external timestamps, civil/offset forms, zoned/IANA/DST-aware forms, localized calendar/locale rendering, storage/migration adapters, and deterministic/fake-clock lanes** distinct instead of narrating one fake universal datetime lane.

- `portable-pty` keeps proving that PTY-backed interaction is a different mode entirely, not just another `stdout` setting.

Archive decision for this revision: add `design/process-surface-lane-map.md` and `design/process-surface-pilot-program.md`; refresh `gaps/process-surfaces-subprocesses-pipelines-and-supervision-contracts.md`, `design/process-surface-kit.md`, and `proposals/epic-process-surface-kit.md`; and strengthen hygiene so future subprocess revisions keep **direct exec, actual shell, shell-like DSLs, pipe graphs, async lifecycle/reaping, tree-control wrappers, and PTY interaction** distinct instead of narrating one fake universal process lane.

## Current frontier correction (rev0320)
### Promote Async Commons Kit beneath Async Lifecycle / Async Reliability and beside Navigation + Commons
Treat **Async Commons Kit** as one of the clearest newly under-modeled seams until the archive can preserve **what async capabilities a crate actually needs, what neutral shared async vocabulary exists today, what adapters lose, and which seams are still only watch-worthy** instead of letting every downstream stack keep re-explaining runtime lock-in in its own words.

Why this now matters:
- Rust’s March 20, 2026 challenges post is unusually direct: async remains a major pain point, many users avoid it because it feels like a different programming model, and library choice can immediately lock a project into one runtime family.
- The same post explicitly recommends not only language improvements but also a more cohesive async ecosystem, even floating the idea that some fundamental async library traits/functions may belong in `std` over time.
- The 2025H1 async goal already said runtime choice and runtime interoperability are core pain points, while the Async Book still says async Rust has compatibility constraints between runtimes and a higher maintenance burden than sync Rust.
- The archive already has strong notes for Async Lifecycle and Async Reliability, but those notes should not have to carry the whole portability story themselves. They need one lower-level seam they can import.

What to prefer next:
- keep **Async Commons Kit** responsible for `async-seam/v0`, `async-capability-profile/v0`, `async-common-surface/v0`, `async-adapter-profile/v0`, `async-vector-set/v0`, `async-readiness-report/v0`, and `async-commons-pack/v0`;
- keep **Async Lifecycle Kit** responsible for ownership/cancellation/shutdown evidence, and keep **Async Reliability Stack** responsible for lifecycle/replay/exploration composition above that seam;
- prefer one I/O seam pilot, one runtime-capability-profile pilot, and one explicit stream/async-sequence `watch` report over another universal runtime abstraction or vague portability badge.


## Current frontier correction (rev0319)
### Sharpen further: Cargo Report Kit needs review-separation receipts, maker-checker honesty, and fallback-review controls
Treat **Cargo Report Kit** as still incomplete until its manual-review carries preserve **who proposed the stronger carry, who reviewed it, who executed it, whether that review was genuinely independent, and which compensating control exists when it was not** instead of collapsing all of that into one generic reviewer field.

Why this still matters:
- Radical Governance’s **maker-checker separation / independent challenge / fallback review** pattern is the clearest import: consequential public carry stops being honest when the same actor can package, request, approve, and execute stronger reuse without one compact separation-of-duties answer.
- DelayBasin’s **dual-control revision / identification-packet** instinct sharpens the same point from another angle: once a carry meaningfully changes the archive’s operative head, the archive needs a durable record of who changed what and which second look made that carry legitimate.
- The current Rust archive already distinguishes review requests, decision witnesses, expected-head guards, contradiction holds, waivers, and promotion gates; without review-separation truth, those artifacts can still hide self-approval theater or vague “review happened somewhere” language.
- Cargo’s growing report/session history makes this more important, not less: replayed reports, build-analysis sessions, projected bundles, and promoted public/frozen heads can now survive long enough that later consumers need to know not just *that* review happened, but whether it cleared an independent checker or only a fallback control.

What to prefer next:
- keep **Cargo Report Kit** responsible for `cargo-report-review-separation-receipt/v0`, maker-checker class truth, compensating-control truth, and fallback-review expiry when review independence is weak;
- keep downstream Build-State / Perf / Feedback consumers above that seam rather than letting them infer that one `reviewer` field means real independent clearance;
- prefer explicit self-review hold or time-bounded fallback controls over letting requester-authored approval silently masquerade as independent review.


## Current frontier correction (rev0318)
### Sharpen further: Cargo Report Kit needs exact review-head guards and rereview triggers
Treat **Cargo Report Kit** as still incomplete until its manual-review carries are **bound to the exact reviewed head** instead of drifting forward whenever a newer operational pack appears in the same lineage.

Why this still matters:
- pyCausalWeave’s **expected-head guard** work is the clearest import: merge/queue/review truth stops being honest when a stale request silently rebinds to a newer head rather than surfacing `stale-head` / `rereview-needed` / `reissue` truth.
- Goldenrule’s **lineage head register + citation head warnings** sharpens the retained-artifact version of the same lesson: operational heads move, frozen/citation heads are stricter, and ambiguity/staleness belongs in a compact durable surface instead of operator memory.
- The current Rust archive already distinguishes operational heads, frozen heads, currentness, contradiction holds, waivers, and decision witnesses; without a review-head guard, one old approval can still impersonate approval of the new head.
- Cargo’s growing report/session history makes this more important, not less: a replayed report id or session-derived pack is historical by construction, so stronger carry should stay exact about *which retained head was actually reviewed*.

What to prefer next:
- keep **Cargo Report Kit** responsible for `expected_head_ref`, `head_lineage_ref`, `reviewed_head_ref`, `head_guard_status`, and explicit `reissue-required` posture when the lineage tip changes;
- keep downstream Build-State / Perf / Feedback consumers above that seam rather than letting them infer that “reviewed once” means “reviewed for the latest head”;
- prefer stale-head hold + explicit reissue over silent carry-forward of approval.


## Current frontier correction (rev0317)
### Sharpen further: Cargo Report Kit needs review requests and decision witnesses
Treat **Cargo Report Kit** as still incomplete until it can preserve **manual-review carries** as first-class request/decision artifacts rather than smoothing them into one queue/status label.

Why this still matters:
- pyCausalWeave’s **task-agent review-request split truth** is the clearest import: queued review, reviewed basis, and merge-ready approval stop being one overloaded field only when the request itself becomes a durable artifact.
- VHK and EvidenceVault reinforce the same lesson operationally: review queues and published/frozen carries need explicit packs/decisions rather than sticky folder state or implied approval.
- Radical Governance and DelayBasin sharpen the boundary from another angle: control surfaces and arbitration witnesses still need one compact answer to what was asked, what basis was consulted, and what exact carry was or was not cleared.
- Cargo Report Kit now already has contradiction packets, waivers, currentness, compatibility, and promotion gates; without a review-request / decision-witness split, those gates can still collapse into vague “reviewed” prose once a human is involved.

What to prefer next:

- keep **Build-State Evidence / Perf Labs / Feedback Loop** above that seam as consumers, not silent sources of review approval;
- prefer explicit request + witness artifacts over letting `pending-review`, `resolved`, or `approved-with-warning` impersonate the full manual-review story.


## Current frontier correction (rev0317)
### Sharpen further: Cargo Report Kit needs contradiction packets, arbitration witnesses, and competing-head hold posture
Treat **Cargo Report Kit** as still incomplete until it can preserve **same-scope competing report stories** as first-class artifacts rather than smoothing them into one winner.

Why this still matters:
- DelayBasin’s new **contradiction-packet / precedence-ladder / conflict-transparent-abstention** and **arbitration-witness / tie-set / confusability-budget** work is a strong warning that once several eligible stories survive local fit checks, honest routing requires preserving the tie and the allowed arbitration rule rather than letting vividness, recency, or convenience silently choose for you.
- Goldenrule’s **lineage-head register** strengthens the same point from another angle: the latest operational head and the citation-grade/frozen head are not always the same object, and branch/ambiguity warnings matter when there is no unique tip.
- EvidenceVault and VHK then sharpen the operational answer: unresolved state should live in an explicit **hold / review queue** surface rather than in folder motion or reviewer memory.
- Cargo’s current report direction makes this directly relevant: the ecosystem can now retain stable future-incompat replay artifacts, nightly build-analysis session imports, replayed timings/rebuilds, copied HTML renderings, and pack-authored summaries side by side, so a portable layer needs one compact answer when two artifacts describe the same package/session/coverage slice differently.
- The missing contribution is therefore not “pick the latest pack and move on.” It is a thin `cargo reportpack` layer that can emit a `cargo-report-contradiction-packet/v0`, preserve the contender set and precedence rule, mark when no automatic winner exists, and block stronger current/public/frozen-head reuse until the conflict is actually resolved.

What to prefer next:
- keep **Cargo Report Kit** responsible for contradiction packets, arbitration witnesses, review-queue hold posture, competing operational-head warnings, and explicit blocked stronger-use rules when same-scope artifacts disagree;
- keep **Build-State Evidence / Perf Labs / Feedback Loop** above that seam as consumers, not silent arbitrators of raw report truth;
- prefer explicit `hold` / `manual-review-required` posture over letting one summary, projected bundle, or later import silently inherit stronger authority than the conflict surface justifies.


## Current frontier correction (rev0314)
### Re-elevate: Cargo Report Kit
Treat **Cargo Report Kit** as one of the clearest near-term raw-evidence seams beneath **Build-State Evidence**, **Perf Labs**, and the emerging **Feedback Loop Stack**.

Why elevate it again now:
- The Cargo Book already documents a stable `cargo report` lane, and today that lane supports `future-incompat` reports.
- The future-incompat docs show Cargo stores report IDs and can replay a full report later, which means Cargo-native report identity is already a real thing rather than only terminal output.
- Cargo’s unstable-features docs now define `-Zbuild-analysis`: Cargo persists JSONL logs under `$CARGO_HOME/log/`, gives each invocation a unique session ID, and exposes `cargo report sessions`, `cargo report timings`, and `cargo report rebuilds` over past sessions.
- The Cargo 1.94 development-cycle update says this family is still moving quickly: timings parity work, new rebuild/session commands, man pages for `cargo report *`, and removal of unstable `--timings=FMT` because report replay is becoming the preferred direction.
- The Cargo build-analysis goal explicitly wants external tools to analyze historical build behavior over time **while keeping collection opt-in, privacy/performance-conscious, and free of user-facing stability guarantees during prototyping**.
- Cross-datacube comparison had already exposed two missing cuts here: **authoritative native basis versus portable/shared/frozen surfaces**, and **observed native scope versus later claimed coverage**.
- A deeper pass across DelayBasin, Goldenrule, pyCausalWeave, EvidenceVault, and The Election Stack exposed the next durable seam: one retained historical artifact must not silently pose as **current active truth**. In Cargo terms, one replayable report id, one old build-analysis session, or one copied timings HTML file should not silently become “the current workspace build state” or “the current compile-time posture” without an explicit observation epoch, currentness class, and recheck rule.
- A still-deeper pass across TriKEM, Radical Governance, AnonSync, and the Cargo sources exposed the next missing cut: **retained or promoted artifacts also need an explicit import-compatibility and material-change discipline**. Because build-analysis is intentionally unstable and Cargo is still changing report commands and adjacent layouts, one pack rendered by decoder version X against nightly/toolchain/schema family Y should not silently inherit the authority of a later toolchain or a changed importer without a reviewed drift classification, compatibility receipt, and fail-closed re-import/re-review gate.
- A fresh pass across **Rust-Crate-Dreams**, **TriKEM**, **EvidenceVault**, and the Cargo docs exposed the next still-missing cut: **authoritative native basis also needs explicit authority-origin / import-path truth, fallback-order posture, and refusal posture**. Because the same review surface can be reached through replayable report ids, direct native session-log bytes, copied native bytes, copied HTML renderings, or pack-authored summaries, one pack should not silently inherit the authority of the strongest path when it was actually produced by a weaker fallback.
- A still-deeper pass across **Radical Governance**, **EvidenceVault**, **DelayBasin**, **SlopOS**, and the Cargo goal text exposed one more seam that actually matters here: **portable/shared packs also need explicit projection receipts**. Because Cargo build-analysis aims to record CLI arguments and invocation metadata while remaining privacy-conscious and locally controlled, a later issue/support/public artifact should not silently hide whether local-private paths, env-derived values, raw payloads, or whole fields were preserved, coarsened, hashed, omitted, or later lost from the native basis.
- A deeper pass across **Goldenrule**, **Radical Governance**, **GlassTTY**, and the now-fixture-backed Cargo-report material exposed the next missing cut: **fixtures alone are not enough if their class meanings remain open strings**. The primary datacube still needed a machine-readable class register, scenario coverage index, and small review-gate surface so later revisions cannot silently relabel claim classes, currentness classes, projection classes, quarantine triggers, promotion targets, or warning classes in prose only.
- One more pass across **SlopOS**, **DelayBasin**, **AnonSync**, and the Rust material exposed the next missing cut: **quarantine-first retained capture plus derived-truth non-laundering**. Because unstable build-analysis imports, decoder-unknown fields, basis-missing attachments, and privacy-sensitive native bytes can still be worth keeping, the right answer cannot always be delete-or-share. The pack needs an explicit `quarantined-retained` / `capture-only` posture where bytes remain historically useful without silently becoming search/snippet/summary/current/public truth, and any later OCR/summary/normalization has to live on a distinct derived artifact instead of semantic back-writing onto the quarantined source.
- A final pass across **Radical Governance**, **Rust-Crate-Dreams**, **Goldenrule**, and the fixture-backed Cargo-report material exposed one more missing cut that still matters here: **explicit waivers need a first-class, expiring ledger**. The primary datacube already talked about “explicit waiver required” for stronger share/current/frozen reuse, but without a waiver ledger those exceptions can quietly become permanent semantics. The pack therefore needs a `cargo-report-waiver-ledger/v0` surface that names the stronger requested use, the weaker safe sentence, the owner/reviewer pair, the expiry clock, and the demotion behavior once that waiver ages out.
- A further comparison pass across **Hyperepo**, **Goldenrule**, **Rust-Crate-Dreams**, and **EvidenceVault** exposed the next still-missing cut: the Rust archive had become strict about **what** a Cargo report pack means, but still too loose about **how** a projected pack, derived summary, or promoted shared surface was actually produced. The same support bundle, summary, or frozen head should not appear without an explicit execution story naming the operation class, tool/importer identity, input refs, output refs, and parent artifacts that were involved.
- The pack therefore now needs a `cargo-report-execution-receipt/v0` plus a small `cargo-report-lineage-register/v0`. The execution receipt should say which operation ran (`import-session`, `pack`, `projection`, `quarantine`, `promote`, `diff`, `explain`, or `derived-summary`), which tool/decoder/reviewer lane produced the artifact, which inputs and outputs were involved, and whether the result is canonical native import, bounded projection, quarantined retention, or a derived explanation. The lineage register should then keep parent/child refs, supersession links, and head-role truth visible so derived artifacts cannot semantic back-write onto their source or quietly inherit a stronger head role than the lineage supports.
- The missing contribution is therefore **not** another dashboard, HTML parser, or build-doctor clone. It is the portable import-and-pack layer above Cargo-native report storage and below build/perf/debug consumers, with one explicit answer to whether a pack is merely historical, current only as-of a named observation, actually rechecked against a newer native basis, already superseded, blocked from stronger reuse because the native lane / decoder / toolchain basis materially drifted, or intentionally retained in quarantine because stronger interpretation/shareability is not yet safe. Just as importantly after the latest comparison pass, that layer now needs a **fixture-backed artifact spine**: once authority paths, projection receipts, quarantine classes, currentness classes, compatibility gates, and promotion warnings exist, the archive should freeze them through schemas, examples, and named review scenarios so later revisions cannot quietly change trust semantics in prose alone.

What to prefer next:

- keep **Cargo Report Kit** responsible for native report-family import, authoritative native basis, authority-origin/import-path truth, fallback-order posture, refusal-posture gates, exact session/report scope, observed coverage slices, lane-wide claim-class honesty, observation-epoch truth, currentness classes, replay/copy provenance, local-private-versus-shared visibility posture, share-safe projection receipts, redaction/omission discipline, retained-basis-loss warnings, quarantine-first retained-capture posture, omitted-field non-inference, derived-artifact separation, frozen-head/promotion discipline, superseded/stale-head warnings, import-compatibility receipts, decoder/toolchain/schema-drift posture, material-change reimport/re-review gates, **fixture-backed schemas/examples/scenario corpus plus taxonomy-backed class registers, scenario-coverage index, machine-readable review gates, machine-checked contract hygiene, expiring waiver ledgers, execution receipts, and derivation lineage registers**, normalized report packs, and consumer-lossiness notes;
- keep **Build-State Evidence Stack** responsible for cache/change/diagnosis meaning above imported report facts;
